### [1] [Scalar-scalar-tensor interaction in DHOST theories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20730)
*S. Mironov,V. Volkova*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper derives the cubic action for cosmological perturbations in quadratic DHOST theories, focusing on scalar-scalar-tensor interactions in unitary gauge, to enable estimation of gravitational wave decay into scalars in late-time dark energy models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable quantitative estimation of gravitational wave decay into scalar excitations—a key phenomenological signature—in quadratic DHOST dark energy models during cosmic late-time evolution.

Method: Derives the general cubic-order action for scalar and tensor perturbations about a cosmological background within the quadratic DHOST class, using a fully covariant formalism and imposing unitary gauge at the perturbative level.

Result: Obtains the complete cubic interaction Lagrangian coupling two scalars and one tensor (i.e., φφhμν), which governs the graviton-to-scalars decay amplitude in these theories.

Conclusion: The derived cubic action provides the essential theoretical foundation for computing gravitational wave decay rates in viable quadratic DHOST dark energy scenarios, linking high-order scalar-tensor structure to observable gravitational-wave phenomenology.

Abstract: We derive the general cubic action for perturbations about a cosmological background in the quadratic subclass of degenerate higher-order scalar-tensor (DHOST) theories, focusing on scalar-scalar-tensor interactions. We adopt a fully covariant formulation and implement unitary gauge at the level of perturbations. This mixed sector provides the key ingredient for estimating the decay rate of a gravitational wave into two scalar excitations in the quadratic DHOST models of dark energy in the late-time Universe.

</details>


### [2] [Study of Cosmic Acceleration of the Universe in the Presence of Bulk Viscous Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20759)
*Dheeraj Singh Rana*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This thesis explores viscous f(Q) gravity—a non-Riemannian extension of General Relativity—to explain cosmic acceleration, derives and constrains exact cosmological solutions using observational data, and assesses model viability via dynamical systems and epoch reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The observed late-time accelerated expansion of the Universe—unexplained by standard GR without dark energy—motivates exploring modified gravity theories; specifically, non-Riemannian f(Q) gravity coupled with bulk viscosity offers a geometric alternative to dark energy.

Method: Analytical derivation of exact cosmological solutions in f(Q) gravity with bulk-viscous fluid; observational constraint of free parameters (e.g., using SNe Ia, H(z), BAO); dynamical systems analysis to study fixed points, stability, and phase-space evolution; comparison against key cosmological epochs (radiation/matter domination, late-time acceleration).

Result: Viscous f(Q) models yield exact solutions consistent with observational data; constrained parameter sets reproduce ΛCDM-like expansion history; several nonlinear f(Q) forms exhibit stable late-time attractors corresponding to accelerated expansion and successfully traverse matter-dominated and dark-energy-dominated epochs.

Conclusion: Bulk-viscous f(Q) gravity is a viable, self-consistent alternative to ΛCDM: it geometrically explains cosmic acceleration without invoking dark energy, satisfies background observational constraints, and supports realistic cosmic evolution across multiple epochs.

Abstract: Over the past century, both theoretical advancements and experimental observations have established General Relativity (GR) as the most successful framework for describing gravitational phenomena. However, observations from multiple cosmological probes over the last two decades have provided compelling evidence for the accelerated expansion of the Universe. The rapid progress in observational astronomy and precision cosmology has highlighted several challenges that motivate the search for extensions or alternatives to General Relativity. In this thesis, we investigate alternative formulations of gravity based on non-Riemannian geometry, with particular emphasis on (f(Q)) gravity in the presence of bulk viscosity. We first explore the existence of exact cosmological solutions for viscous fluid models within the framework of (f(Q)) gravity and constrain the free parameters of these solutions using observational datasets. The resulting constrained models are then employed to study the evolutionary history of cosmic expansion. Our analysis demonstrates that the proposed viscous (f(Q)) gravity models can successfully account for the observed late-time acceleration of the Universe. Furthermore, we examine several classes of nonlinear viscous (f(Q)) gravity models through both dynamical systems and observational analyses. Particular attention is given to the cosmological viability of these models and their ability to reproduce the different evolutionary epochs of the Universe, ranging from matter-dominated eras to accelerated expansion phases.

</details>


### [3] [Selective enhancement of quantum decay channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20766)
*Pritam Nanda,Kinjalk Lochan*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper shows that cavity boundary conditions enhance low-energy quantum decay rates by boosting infrared-mode support and resonance, offering an alternative to high-energy experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the suppression of low-energy quantum decays in free space due to vanishing mode density in the infrared, and to explore controllable enhancement via engineered electromagnetic environments.

Method: Theoretical analysis of quantum field decay rates under cavity boundary conditions, comparing mode density distributions and transition probability densities between free space and confined geometries, with emphasis on resonant cavity effects.

Result: Cavity confinement significantly enhances low-energy decay rates by increasing infrared-mode availability and enabling resonant amplification; optimal cavity geometry acts as a 'low-energy amplifier' for otherwise suppressed processes.

Conclusion: Controlled boundary conditions—particularly resonant cavities—provide a viable, experimentally accessible route to amplify rare or infrared-suppressed quantum decays, with implications for precision QED tests and searches for exotic physics.

Abstract: In the decay of quantum particles under field theoretic consideration, the decay rate is typically a convolution of the density of modes the primary field is allowed to decay into and the allowed probability density for the field to decay into such modes. In free space, though many such processes show high amplitude of such transitions towards the infrared sector, the depletion of allowed mode density in that regime arrests the efficacy of such decays at low energies. Therefore in free space, in order to enhance the decay rate, one needs the transition probability density to be rich enough towards the high energy sector where mode density support is also high enough to make the rate sufficiently large. In this work we argue that in the controlled boundary condition environment e.g. in a cavity, the mode functions of product field receive significant support towards their infrared sector, boosting the probability (and hence rates) of low energy processes. The cavity geometry offers sweet spots in terms of resonant geometry around which the interaction of a primary field with product fields receives dramatic enhancement, significantly enlarging its decay rates. Therefore, a judicious selection of cavity geometry serves as a potential substitute to studying interesting processes at high energy. The results have direct relevance for the study of QED processes and implications for the study of exotic new physics are also discussed.

</details>


### [4] [The Cosmological Constant Problem: An Accessible Introduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20778)
*Ali Kaya,Adam Lahey*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper provides a pedagogical, regularization-aware analysis of the cosmological constant problem using a massive real scalar field in flat and expanding spacetimes, highlighting both the magnitude of the vacuum energy mismatch and conceptual issues like vacuum non-uniqueness and scale choices in cosmology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the cosmological constant problem by carefully accounting for regularization and renormalization in quantum vacuum energy calculations—moving beyond the oversimplified 120-order-of-magnitude discrepancy—and to highlight underappreciated conceptual challenges in curved spacetime QFT.

Method: Computes vacuum energy density and pressure for a massive real scalar field using dimensional regularization, hard momentum cutoff, and adiabatic regularization, both in Minkowski spacetime and in a classical Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) background; treats gravity classically and omits quantum gravity effects.

Result: Demonstrates that while the naive 120-orders-of-magnitude discrepancy is regularization-dependent and misleading, proper QFT treatment still yields a severe, physically meaningful mismatch between predicted vacuum energy and observed dark energy; additionally identifies conceptual ambiguities—e.g., vacuum state definition, comoving vs. physical scales—in expanding spacetime.

Conclusion: The cosmological constant problem remains profound not only quantitatively but also conceptually; rigorous QFT in curved spacetime reveals inherent ambiguities (e.g., vacuum non-uniqueness) that must be addressed alongside regularization and renormalization, even without invoking quantum gravity.

Abstract: We present a pedagogical introduction to the cosmological constant problem that requires only basic knowledge of quantum field theory and general relativity. A massive real scalar field is used to illustrate how the quantum vacuum energy density and pressure can be calculated both in flat spacetime and in an expanding universe. Detailed computations are provided for dimensional, cutoff, and adiabatic regularizations. No attempt is made to address quantum gravitational effects, and the expanding-universe background is treated classically. We point out that although the commonly cited discrepancy of 120 orders of magnitude between theory and observation is based on an estimate that does not account for regularization and renormalization, fundamental principles of quantum field theory nevertheless lead to a huge mismatch. In addition to this large discrepancy, we emphasize that there are also conceptual challenges related to cosmic expansion, such as the choice between comoving and physical scales in certain contexts and the non-uniqueness of vacuum.

</details>


### [5] [$\mathrm{ISIM}(2)$ gravitational gauge model via Soft Algebra formalism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20806)
*J. M. B. Matzenbacher,J. M. Hoff da Silva*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper constructs a torsionless gravitational model by gauging the ISIM(2) Lorentz subgroup using the soft algebra formalism, deriving a closed form for its Lie algebra 𝔰𝔦𝔪(2) and its inhomogeneous extension, and obtaining an invariant action.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formulate a consistent gravitational theory based on the ISIM(2) symmetry—a physically relevant subgroup of the Lorentz group—using the soft algebra approach to accommodate non-semisimple structure and enable gauge-theoretic gravity construction.

Method: Soft algebra formalism applied to the ISIM(2) group; derivation of closed algebraic structure for 𝔰𝔦𝔪(2) and its inhomogeneous extension 𝔦𝔰𝔦𝔪(2); construction of a torsionless Cartan geometry; derivation of a diffeomorphism- and local ISIM(2)-invariant action.

Result: A complete, torsionless gravitational model with local ISIM(2) symmetry, including explicit algebraic structure, geometric setup, and a fully invariant action functional.

Conclusion: The soft algebra formalism successfully enables the gauging of the non-semisimple ISIM(2) group, yielding a viable, geometrically consistent gravitational theory that extends beyond standard Poincaré or (A)dS gauge approaches.

Abstract: This work presents a comprehensive account of a gravitational model based on the gauging of the $\mathrm{ISIM}(2)$ Lorentz subgroup parameters through the so-called soft algebra formalism. After presenting a closed form for $\mathfrak{sim}(2)$ and its inhomogeneous extension, we proceed to develop a complete torsionless gravitational model, culminating in an invariant action.

</details>


### [6] [Exact solutions using power law scalar potential in the Saez-Ballester-K-essence like theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20894)
*J. Socorro,A. Gil-Ocaranza,Ximena López-Mujica,Cesar Aarón Pacheco-Vázquez*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper constructs a K-essence cosmological model using a negative power-law Sáez–Ballester potential, maps it via field redefinition to a solvable FLRW system, obtains exact classical solutions showing late-time de Sitter-like acceleration (q → −1), derives and solves the Wheeler–DeWitt equation exactly, yielding a consistent classical–quantum description where the scalar field acts as a fixed cosmic background.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To construct a tractable K-essence model admitting both exact classical and quantum cosmological solutions, bridging classical accelerated expansion with quantum consistency.

Method: Field redefinition (φ → φ) to map the K-essence action onto a known integrable FLRW model; Hamiltonian formalism for classical solution; canonical quantization leading to the Wheeler–DeWitt equation and its exact analytical solution.

Result: Exact classical solutions for scale factor and scalar field exhibiting late-time accelerated expansion with q → −1; exact analytical solutions of the Wheeler–DeWitt equation; quantum solutions indicate the scalar field serves as a fixed background rather than a dynamical degree of freedom.

Conclusion: The model provides a rare fully solvable K-essence framework with consistent classical dynamics and quantum description, reinforcing the role of the scalar field as a non-dynamical cosmic stage in the quantum regime.

Abstract: We investigate a K-essence like cosmological model whose scalar-field potential is constructed from a negative power-law Sáez--Ballester potential. By means of a suitable field redefinition from $φ$ to $\varphi$, we show that the resulting field equations acquire a mathematical structure analogous to that of a previously solved Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker (FLRW) cosmological model. This correspondence allows us to obtain exact classical solutions for both the scale factor and the scalar field within the Hamiltonian formalism. The resulting cosmological dynamics exhibits a late-time accelerated expansion, with the deceleration parameter approaching the asymptotic value $q\rightarrow -1$, characteristic of a de Sitter phase. At the quantum level, the corresponding Wheeler-DeWitt (WDW) equation is derived and exact quantum solutions are obtained. These results provide a consistent classical and quantum description of the cosmological evolution generated by this class of K-essence models. In this formalism, the scalar field remains as a cosmic background where the universe unfolds, which is glimpsed from the quantum solution perspective.

</details>


### [7] [Scattering, Hawking Radiation and Neutrino Energy Deposition in Euler-Heisenberg Black Holes Surrounded by Perfect Fluid Dark Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20931)
*Ramon Becar,P. A. Gonzalez,Ali Ovgun,Joel Saavedra,Yerko Vasquez*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper investigates scattering and dynamical properties of an Euler-Heisenberg black hole immersed in perfect fluid dark matter (PFDM), analyzing quasinormal modes, greybody factors, absorption cross sections, Hawking radiation, and neutrino-antineutrino annihilation—finding PFDM contracts optical structure and enhances damping, while Euler-Heisenberg effects are weaker and charge-dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically compare how nonlinear electrodynamics (via Euler-Heisenberg correction) and dark-matter environments (via perfect fluid dark matter halo) jointly influence black hole perturbations, scattering, and emission properties—beyond standard vacuum or pure-charge models.

Method: Analyzes scalar, electromagnetic, and effective axial spin-2 perturbations on a fixed Euler-Heisenberg + PFDM black hole background; computes quasinormal modes using 13th-order WKB + Padé resummation; derives near-extremal purely imaginary modes analytically; obtains exact greybody factors via numerical integration of radial wave equations; calculates absorption cross sections, Hawking spectra, and relativistic enhancement of neutrino-antineutrino annihilation.

Result: PFDM parameter contracts photon sphere, increases QNM oscillation frequency and damping rate, suppresses transmission, and yields a spin-independent purely imaginary QNM branch in near-horizon limit; Euler-Heisenberg correction causes milder near-horizon deformation, significant only at large charge; analytical lower bounds for greybody factors are established and numerically validated.

Conclusion: The combined Euler-Heisenberg + PFDM framework provides a unified scattering description where dark-matter environment dominates dynamical and observational signatures over nonlinear electrodynamics—except at high charge—enabling discrimination between astrophysical and quantum-corrected black hole models.

Abstract: We study the dynamical and scattering properties for the Euler-Heisenberg BH surrounded by perfect fluid dark matter. The geometry contains a compact non-linear electrodynamic correction governed by the EH coupling and a logarithmic dark-matter contribution governed by the surrounding PFDM halo. We study the scalar, electromagnetic and a particular effective axial spin-2 channel constructed on the fixed EH plus PFDM background, acting as a proxy for the gravitational-like perturbation problem and not for the fully coupled gravitational perturbation problem. We compute the quasinormal-mode spectrum employing a thirteenth-order WKB method supplemented with Padé resummation and compare it with the eikonal prediction calculated in terms of the angular frequency of the photon sphere and the Lyapunov exponent. Moreover, we study the near-extremal configurations and derive a purely imaginary branch of quasinormal frequencies in the near-horizon region, whose damping rate increases with the PFDM parameter and is nearly spin-independent. We then compute exact greybody factors by direct numerical integration of the radial wave equation and compare them to analytical lower bounds. We also analyze the absorption cross sections and the Hawking emission spectra. We also calculate the relativistic enhancement of the neutrino-antineutrino annihilation channel outside the EHPFDM black hole. We find that the PFDM parameter contracts the optical structure, increases the oscillation frequency, enhances the damping rate and suppresses transmission. On the other hand, the Euler-Heisenberg correction leads to a weaker near-horizon deformation whose effect becomes relevant for sufficiently large charge. These results provide a common scattering framework for comparing the impact of dark-matter environments and nonlinear electrodynamics

</details>


### [8] [Quantum Dust from the Curse of Dimensionality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20943)
*Kenan Oggad*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper identifies a kinematic, geometry-of-states explanation—via concentration of measure on the Fubini-Study manifold—for why diverse quantum gravity approaches consistently yield an effective 2D spacetime at short scales; it argues this 2D reading arises not from intrinsic geometry but from diffusion probing of an equidistant 'dust' state ensemble, and introduces 'spectral faithfulness' as a criterion to distinguish measurement artifacts from genuine dimensional structure using low-lying Laplacian eigenvalue density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the ubiquitous emergence of two-dimensional spacetime across disparate quantum gravity theories—not via dynamical mechanisms specific to each theory, but through a universal, kinematic feature of the geometry of quantum state space.

Method: Applies concentration of measure on the Fubini-Study manifold of pure quantum states to show that high-dimensional state spaces force random finite samples into equidistant configurations ('dust'); analyzes diffusion-based spectral dimension estimation on such dust; formalizes the distinction between artifact and genuine dimension via low-eigenvalue asymptotics of the Laplacian; verifies core results in Lean 4 using Mathlib and the Beta distribution of state overlaps.

Result: Demonstrates that a diffusion probe applied to an equidistant dust yields spectral dimension 2 independent of underlying geometry; establishes that only the asymptotic density of near-zero Laplacian eigenvalues (not a single relaxation scale) determines genuine dimension; classifies spectral behaviors—power-law tail (genuine d), gapped single scale (artifactual 2), and two-scale band (universal line)—and machine-verifies them.

Conclusion: The recurrent value 2 for the short-scale spectral dimension is generically an artifact of state-space geometry and measurement protocol, not evidence of fundamental 2D spacetime; genuine dimensional content requires spectral faithfulness—i.e., a power-law eigenvalue density near zero—and its preservation under emergence maps remains an open condition.

Abstract: Why do unrelated approaches to quantum gravity nearly all find spacetime two-dimensional at the shortest scales? Each theory answers only within its own dynamics; we highlight a single kinematic route to the same value, one assuming no field equation and living in the geometry of the space of states alone. That route is concentration of measure on the Fubini-Study geometry of pure states, which forces the pairwise distances of a random sample to equalize as the dimension grows, so any finite sample collapses to an equidistant dust whose thresholded metric graph is the complete graph. Handed this dust, a diffusion probe reads it as two-dimensional in the large-sample limit, the value the running spectral dimension takes at the dust's single relaxation scale, a property of the measurement rather than the structure; this convergence on two is not, by itself, evidence that spacetime is two-dimensional. Whether a given two is such an artifact is governed by the Laplacian spectrum near zero, and whether that reading carries across an emergence map is the condition we call spectral faithfulness; a single relaxation scale encodes no spectral dimension that tells one structure from another. The collapse, the probe value, and the eigenvalue-density criterion are machine-checked in Lean 4 against Mathlib, resting on the standard Beta law of overlaps; a power-law tail of small eigenvalues reads a genuine dimension, a single scale above a gap reads two at its own clock, and a gapped two-scale band reads off the universal line. These classes are run on graph-Laplacian proxies, and whether a link-graph reading carries to the physical nonlocal operator is left open. The spectral test reads the eigenvalue density near zero and separates, on a given structure, a measurement artifact from a dimension the structure genuinely expresses.

</details>


### [9] [Out-of-Equilibrium Effects in Non-Radial Relativistic Stellar Perturbations: A Model-Agnostic Formulation and Mode Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20957)
*Takuya Katagiri,Victor Guedes,Kent Yagi*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper develops a general, model-agnostic framework for analyzing non-radial oscillations of relativistic stars with out-of-equilibrium effects (e.g., viscosity, thermal conductivity), extending the Lindblom-Detweiler formalism to both even- and odd-parity perturbations without assuming specific constitutive relations; it applies the framework to the Bemfica-Disconzi-Noronha-Kovtun fluid, computing mode frequency/damping shifts and identifying new eigenmode families arising from the structure of the dissipative eigenvalue problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically understand how generic nonequilibrium corrections—beyond perfect-fluid assumptions—affect non-radial oscillations of relativistic stars, especially in regimes where transport coefficients (e.g., shear/bulk viscosity, thermal conductivity) become dynamically relevant.

Method: Extends the Lindblom-Detweiler formalism to incorporate generic, linearized, first-order dissipative corrections in the stress-energy tensor via thermodynamic decomposition and tensorial classification; constructs gauge-invariant perturbation equations for both even- and odd-parity sectors without specifying constitutive relations; applies the formalism perturbatively to the Bemfica-Disconzi-Noronha-Kovtun causal relativistic fluid model.

Result: Derives a unified, model-agnostic perturbation framework revealing how dissipative corrections couple to geometric and fluid variables; computes leading-order corrections to quasi-normal mode frequencies and damping times; identifies structural features (e.g., extended eigensystem dimensionality) enabling new mode families absent in perfect-fluid theory.

Conclusion: The framework establishes a rigorous, thermodynamically consistent foundation for studying stellar seismology beyond perfect fluids, enabling direct comparison across relativistic dissipative theories and paving the way for constraining transport properties from gravitational-wave or electromagnetic asteroseismology observations.

Abstract: We present a systematic, model-agnostic analysis of out-of-equilibrium effects, including viscosity and thermal conductivity, in non-radial oscillations of relativistic stars. Extending the Lindblom-Detweiler formalism, we construct, to our knowledge, the first general framework for linear, non-radial relativistic stellar perturbations that incorporates generic nonequilibrium corrections to the perfect-fluid sector in both the even- and odd-parity channels. Our framework is formulated in terms of the tensorial structure and thermodynamic decomposition of generic corrections without relying on any specific constitutive relations, thereby allowing us to elucidate, at a structural level, how these effects enter the perturbation equations and contribute to geometric deformations and fluid fluctuations. As an application, we consider the Bemfica-Disconzi-Noronha-Kovtun fluid and perturbatively investigate shifts in the frequencies and damping times of modes connected to their perfect-fluid counterparts in the limit of vanishing transport coefficients. We also identify structural features of the closed eigenvalue problem that can give rise to additional mode families. Our formalism provides a unified framework for analyzing how different relativistic fluid theories modify the structure of non-radial stellar perturbations.

</details>


### [10] [Solving Einstein Field Equations on a Digital Quantum Computer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20975)
*Clelia Altomonte,Malcolm Fairbairn*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper proposes a quantum algorithm for solving Einstein Field Equations within the WEBB tetrad formalism of Numerical Relativity, demonstrates it on Schwarzschild black hole evolution and quasinormal mode extraction, and implements it on Qiskit simulators and IBM hardware via NQCC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore quantum computing's potential for accelerating computationally intensive simulations in Numerical Relativity—specifically solving partial differential equations governing spacetime dynamics—where classical methods face scalability bottlenecks.

Method: Develops a proof-of-principle quantum algorithm tailored to the WEBB tetrad formalism; encodes the Einstein Field Equations into a quantum linear system; implements components using Qiskit; validates on classical simulators and IBM quantum devices through the UKRI NQCC Quantum Access program; quantifies resource usage (qubits, gates, runtime) and fidelity.

Result: Successful quantum simulation of Schwarzschild spacetime evolution and extraction of gravitational quasinormal modes; empirical characterization of algorithm performance, noise sensitivity, and hardware resource requirements on current NISQ devices.

Conclusion: Quantum algorithms for Numerical Relativity are feasible in principle and demonstrable on near-term hardware, though practical advantage requires error mitigation, improved qubit coherence, and scalable circuit compilation strategies.

Abstract: In this work, we show how simulations performed on classical computers such as those of Numerical Relativity can be tackled by quantum algorithms for solving systems of partial differential equations. We develop a proof-of-principle quantum algorithm for solving Einstein Field Equations in the Wahlquist-Estabrook-Buchman-Bardeen(WEBB) tetrad Numerical Relativity formalism [1], and test it by evolving the Schwarzschild Black Hole spacetime in the WEBB Numerical Relativity formalism [2], perturbing it to obtain gravitational Quasinormal Modes [3]. We program the algorithm components for a gate-based, digital quantum computer using the Qiskit software [4] and run it on classical simulators and physical IBM quantum computers through the UKRI National Quantum Computing Centre (NQCC) Quantum Access program and quantify the computational resources and runtime.

</details>


### [11] [Probing the Circular Unruh Effect with Cavity-Controlled Lamb Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21019)
*Yan Peng,Jiawei Hu,Hongwei Yu*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper proposes using cavity-modified Lamb shift spectroscopy of a centripetally accelerated atom to detect the Unruh effect at ultralow accelerations (~0.5 m/s²), bypassing the conventional requirement of ~10²⁰ m/s².


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct observation of the Unruh effect is hindered by the extreme accelerations (~10²⁰ m/s²) needed to produce measurable Unruh temperatures; a practical, low-acceleration detection scheme is needed.

Method: Theoretical analysis of the Lamb shift of an atom undergoing uniform circular motion inside a high-Q optical cavity; calculation of how cavity boundary conditions modify the electromagnetic density of states and amplify or suppress noninertial (Unruh-related) contributions to the shift.

Result: Cavity tuning enables orders-of-magnitude enhancement of the Unruh-induced Lamb shift; a measurable shift (~10 Hz) arises at accelerations as low as 0.5 m/s² — over 20 orders of magnitude below the standard Unruh scale.

Conclusion: Cavity-controlled Lamb-shift spectroscopy provides a feasible, experimentally accessible pathway to test the circular Unruh effect in the ultralow-acceleration regime.

Abstract: The Unruh effect predicts that accelerated observers perceive the inertial vacuum as populated by particles, providing a flat-spacetime analogue of Hawking radiation. Its direct observation, however, remains experimentally challenging, since an Unruh temperature of $1\,\mathrm{K}$ requires accelerations of order $10^{20}\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$. Here, we show that the Lamb shift of a centripetally accelerated atom inside a high-$Q$ cavity provides a sensitive spectroscopic probe of the Unruh effect at dramatically lower accelerations. The cavity reshapes the electromagnetic density of states and converts otherwise tiny noninertial corrections into tunable level shifts. Depending on the atomic angular velocity and cavity detuning, the Lamb shift can be enhanced, strongly quenched, or completely screened. Remarkably, for experimentally realistic parameters, a rotation-induced shift of order $10\;\mathrm{Hz}$ can arise already at accelerations as low as $0.5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, more than twenty orders of magnitude below the acceleration scale conventionally associated with direct Unruh detection. These results identify cavity-controlled Lamb-shift spectroscopy as a viable route toward laboratory tests of the circular Unruh effect in the ultralow-acceleration regime.

</details>


### [12] [An open-source numerical tool for rational orbits and gravitational radiation in static spherically symmetric spacetimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21029)
*Dan Li,Shiyang Hu,Chen Deng,Shijie Tan,Guansheng He*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Mathematica-based, OpenMP-parallelized computational framework to simulate rational timelike orbits and associated gravitational radiation in static spherically symmetric spacetimes, validated on Schwarzschild geometry and applied to extreme mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) involving Sgr A*.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a versatile, efficient, and user-friendly numerical tool for studying timelike geodesics and gravitational wave emission in generic static spherically symmetric spacetimes—enabling exploration of strong-field gravity and EMRI observability.

Method: Develops a parallelized (OpenMP) Mathematica package that takes an arbitrary covariant metric as input and computes rational orbital configurations, effective potentials, stability criteria, gravitational wave polarizations (h+, h×), and characteristic strain spectra.

Result: The code successfully reproduces known Schwarzschild orbit properties (e.g., ISCO, stable/unstable regions, rational vs. irrational orbits) and predicts detectable characteristic strain amplitudes (~10⁻²¹–10⁻²⁰) for IMBH–Sgr A* EMRIs with LISA-like sensitivity.

Conclusion: The framework serves as a robust, general-purpose tool for relativistic orbit modeling and GW signal prediction in static spherical geometries, bridging theoretical GR and future GW astronomy—particularly for space-based detectors targeting EMRIs.

Abstract: Timelike orbits constitute a crucial probe for exploring the intrinsic properties of curved spacetimes, and the carried gravitational radiation signals provide a direct window into strong field gravity. In this paper, we develop a versatile computational framework based on Mathematica and the OpenMP parallel architecture to simulate the rational orbits of timelike particles and their gravitational radiation in static spherically symmetric spacetimes. Specifically, requiring only the user defined covariant metric, this numerical tool can efficiently calculate rational orbits across various configurations, as well as the corresponding gravitational wave polarization states and characteristic strains. The package presented here offers a highly efficient and comprehensive one-stop solution for investigating the properties of curved spacetimes and their potential observational signatures. To demonstrate the reliability and capability of our code, we apply it to the Schwarzschild spacetime as a test case, illustrating the functionality of the code across several key aspects, including the effective potential, stable orbital regions, rational and irrational orbits, and gravitational wave signals. Furthermore, we show that the gravitational waves emitted by an extreme mass ratio inspiral system composed of an intermediate mass black hole and the Galactic Center supermassive black hole have the potential to be identified by future space detectors.

</details>


### [13] [Population-level correlations in Bayesian statistics: an illustrative model for gravitational-wave astronomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21076)
*Caroline B. Owen,Alexandre Toubiana,Davide Gerosa*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper develops an analytical Gaussian model to examine how single-event parameter correlations and waveform systematic biases affect the inference of population-level correlations in gravitational-wave astrophysics, highlighting risks of false-positive correlation detections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how single-event parameter correlations and waveform systematics impact the reliability of population-level correlation measurements in gravitational-wave catalogs, especially for astrophysical interpretation and tests of general relativity.

Method: Constructs an idealized analytical Gaussian model that jointly treats single-event likelihood correlations and catalog-wide systematic biases, then quantifies their effects on population-level correlation inference using statistical propagation and bias absorption analysis.

Result: Single-event correlations increase uncertainty in inferred population correlations; waveform systematics correlated across events can be absorbed by population models and misinterpreted as genuine astrophysical correlations—leading to false positives.

Conclusion: Population-level correlation inferences are vulnerable to both statistical (single-event) and systematic (waveform modeling) effects; caution and diagnostic tools are needed to distinguish true astrophysical correlations from artifacts.

Abstract: With increasingly large numbers of gravitational-wave events, population inference is now beginning to move beyond predictions of marginal distributions and to probe correlations between compact-binary parameters such as masses, spins, and redshifts. These correlations have strong constraining power for both astrophysics and tests of general relativity. In this paper, we present an idealized analytical model to study the interplay between single-event correlations, systematic biases, and population-level correlations. With this, we investigate the potential emergence of false-positive measurements of population-level correlations. We quantify how the presence of correlations at the single-event level between a pair of parameters increases the uncertainty of population-level correlations for those parameters, potentially obscuring the true underlying population correlation if present. We also find that if waveform systematics lead to biases that are correlated across the catalog (which is likely, because certain regions of the parameter space are more difficult to model), this can be effectively absorbed by a population analysis that targets correlations and can be misinterpreted as such. This simple Gaussian-based model may serve as a broad compass for future, more detailed explorations.

</details>


### [14] [Reconstructing the slope of a nearly flat quintessence potential from cosmography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21127)
*Saikat Chakraborty,Peter K. S. Dunsby,Robert J. Scherrer*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper revisits thawing quintessence models with nearly flat potentials using cosmography, showing that slow-roll conditions enable reconstructing the potential slope from only the deceleration parameter q and Ω_φ—bypassing need for higher-order jerk j—but DESI DR2 data reveal tension with near-flatness; it also identifies attractor behavior in w–Ω_φ and w–w′ planes, links thawing evolution to the q–j plane, and notes viable trajectories cluster near ΛCDM’s j = 1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reassess thawing quintessence models with nearly flat scalar-field potentials within a cosmographic framework, particularly to determine whether slower-roll assumptions relax observational requirements (e.g., avoiding need for jerk j), and to test consistency with recent DESI DR2 data.

Method: Analytical cosmographic analysis: derives relationships between the quintessence potential slope λ = −(dV/dφ)/V and cosmographic parameters (q, j) under slow-roll conditions; examines phase-space dynamics in w–Ω_φ, w–w′, and q–j planes; confronts theoretical predictions with DESI DR2 cosmographic constraints.

Result: Slow-roll conditions allow λ reconstruction from q and Ω_φ alone (no j required); DESI DR2 data suggest tension with near-flat-potential assumption; thawing models exhibit attractor behavior in w–Ω_φ and w–w′ planes, implying universal early-time w ≈ −1 evolution; multiple expansion histories map to same thawing trajectory in q–j plane; all viable trajectories lie close to ΛCDM’s j = 1.

Conclusion: While slow-roll simplifies cosmographic inference for thawing quintessence, observational data challenge the near-flat-potential approximation; the identified attractors and q–j degeneracy highlight limitations of cosmography in distinguishing dark energy models, reinforcing proximity to ΛCDM as a robust feature.

Abstract: We revisit thawing quintessence models with nearly flat scalar-field potentials using a cosmographic framework. Earlier work indicates that the cosmographic reconstruction of the slope $λ=-(dV/dφ)/V$ of the quintessence potential in the general case requires the knowledge of the cosmographic paremeters up to the jerk parameter $j$. In this work we show that the slow-roll conditions $[(dV/dφ)/V]^2 \ll 1$ and $|(d^2V/dφ^2)/V| \ll 1$ allow the reconstruction of the slope of a nearly flat potential with knowledge of only the deceleration parameter $q$ (and the density parameter $Ω_φ$). Confronting the assumption of near-flatness with the cosmographic data after DESI DR2, however, reveals possible tension between the two. We further show that these models exhibit attractor behaviour in the $w$--$Ω_φ$ and $w$--$w'$ phase planes, corresponding to a universal thawing evolution with $w \approx -1$ at early times. We also derive the corresponding relation in the cosmographic $q$--$j$ plane and show that different cosmological expansion histories can produce the same thawing evolution. Nevertheless, all viable trajectories remain close to the $Λ$CDM limit $j=1$.

</details>


### [15] [Entanglement and firewalls in quantum circuit model of black hole evaporation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21181)
*Yi-Chen Lu,Tien Hsieh,Da-Shin Lee*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper revisits a quantum circuit model of black hole evaporation with tripartite systems (BH, JR, ER), introducing a tunable scrambling parameter θ to generalize initial BH qubit states; it analyzes how quantum monogamy governs firewall emergence between BH and radiation, showing θ-dependent firewall timing and robust information recovery via unitary dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the role of quantum monogamy in firewall formation during black hole evaporation by generalizing the initial black hole state using a tunable scrambling parameter θ, beyond the fixed scrambling assumption of prior work.

Method: Extends the tripartite quantum circuit model (BH–JR–ER) by applying a single-parameter scrambling unitary U(θ) to infalling matter qubits; analytically studies entanglement structure, firewall conditions, and information recovery as functions of θ, black hole mass M, and Hawking frequency ω.

Result: Firewall formation between BH and JR occurs earlier for maximal scrambling (θ = π/2) than for no scrambling (θ = 0); a firewall emerges for all Mω when θ lies in an analytically determined range; unitary dynamics enables full retrieval of initial BH qubit state from final radiation, recovering information previously hidden behind the horizon.

Conclusion: Quantum monogamy—modulated by initial scrambling strength θ—controls the onset and structure of firewalls; multipartite entanglement evolution is highly sensitive to initial state preparation, supporting unitary information recovery in black hole evaporation within this circuit model.

Abstract: We reexamine the quantum circuit model of black hole evaporation proposed in Class. Quantum Grav. 35, 235013 (2018). This model incorporates the tripartite systems: black hole, just radiation and early radiation. We apply the scrambling unitary matrix with a single parameter $θ$ to the ground state of the qubits in infalling matter towards a black hole in order to generate initial qubit states of the black hole that are more general than those in Class. Quantum Grav. 35, 235013 (2018). Specifically, the scrambling unitary matrix reduces to no scrambling and maximum scrambling when $θ=0$ and $θ=π/2$, respectively. Our aim is to explore the role of quantum monogamy in the firewall formation between the black hole and radiation. In this model, entanglement and firewall formation depend on the black hole mass $M$ and the frequency of Hawking radiation $ω$. For the initial state with $θ=π/2$, a firewall emerges at an earlier stage of the evolution than with $θ=0$. We also find that a firewall structure emerges between BH and JR, and that the information is carried away by radiation for all values of $Mω$, provided that $θ$ lies within a certain analytically determined range. Following the unitary gate dynamics, the initial black hole qubit state can be retrieved from its imprint on the final radiation state, which was originally hidden behind the black hole's horizon. These results may provide insight into the properties of multipartite entanglement due to the different initial states in the evolution of a quantum circuit model for black hole evaporation.

</details>


### [16] [Temperature of free gravitational field: A geometrical perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21183)
*Samarjit Chakraborty,Gareth Amery,Sunil D Maharaj,Rituparno Goswami*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper introduces a geometric framework linking thermodynamic temperature of the free gravitational field to the non-affinity of null geodesics in a Newman–Penrose tetrad, unifying several known temperature concepts (Clifton–Ellis–Tavakol, Hawking, Unruh) in appropriate limits; extends the result beyond local rotational symmetry to Petrov type D spacetimes (e.g., Kerr); derives hyperbolic wave equations governing temperature evolution, sourced by Weyl curvature and matter; and interprets non-affinity as encoding gravitational redshift/blueshift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify disparate notions of gravitational temperature (e.g., Hawking, Unruh, Clifton–Ellis–Tavakol) under a single geometric principle grounded in null geodesic structure and curvature.

Method: A novel geometric approach using Newman–Penrose formalism and non-affinity of real null geodesics; analysis within local rotational symmetry (LRS) class I spacetimes, extended to Petrov type D (e.g., Kerr); derivation of transport equations via causal propagation and curvature decomposition.

Result: A unified temperature function tied to null geodesic non-affinity; hyperbolic wave equation with Weyl- and matter-sourced forcing term; extension to Kerr; physical interpretation linking temperature to gravitational redshift/blueshift along null rays.

Conclusion: Thermodynamic temperature of the free gravitational field is fundamentally encoded in the non-affine parametrization of null geodesics — a purely geometric, observer-independent quantity — providing a covariant, curvature-based foundation for gravitational thermodynamics across diverse spacetimes.

Abstract: In this paper, using a novel geometrical approach, we relate the concept of the thermodynamic temperature of the free gravitational field, to the non-affinity of real null geodesics in a Newman Penrose tetrad. This naturally links various temperature functions like Clifton, Ellis and Tavakol temperature, Hawking temperature, Unruh temperature etc., in their respective proper limits. Although our analysis is done within the realm of local rotational symmetry, we show that the result can be extended to other Petrov type D geometries, like the Kerr spacetime. We also obtain the geometrical and causal transport equations for this temperature function, in the form of a hyperbolic wave equation with a forcing term, sourced by Weyl curvature and matter. Finally, as a possible physical interpretation of the non-affinity, we relate the geometrical temperature with the gravitational red/blue shift of light rays travelling along null geodesics.

</details>


### [17] [Thermal Stability and QNMs of a Hairy Black Hole in the Presence of a Monopole Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21332)
*George Koutsoumbas,Andri Machattou,Eleftherios Papantonopoulos*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper investigates thermal and dynamical stability of a dilatonic monopole-charged black hole generalizing the GHS-GM solution; it finds enhanced thermal stability with decreasing dilaton scalar charge, and quasi-normal mode analysis confirms linear stability (all imaginary parts negative, overtone decay faster than fundamental).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the stability—both thermal and dynamical—of a generalized GHS-GM black hole coupled to a dilaton and magnetic monopole field, motivated by the role of scalar hair and gauge fields in black hole thermodynamics and perturbative stability.

Method: Thermodynamic analysis (temperature, heat capacity) and linear perturbation theory via quasi-normal mode (QNM) computation of scalar field perturbations around the black hole background.

Result: Thermal stability increases as dilaton scalar charge decreases; QNM spectrum shows monotonically decreasing real and imaginary parts of frequencies with increasing black hole charge; all imaginary parts are negative, overtone modes decay faster than fundamental modes.

Conclusion: The dilatonic monopole-charged black hole is both thermally and dynamically stable under scalar perturbations, with stability strengthened by smaller dilaton scalar charge and larger black hole charge.

Abstract: We study the stability of a generalization of the GHS-GM black hole in the presence of a dilaton and a monopole field. We find that the thermal behaviour of system depends on the scalar charge of the dilaton field and as this parameter is decreasing the system becomes more thermally stable. We also find that, as the charge of the black hole is increasing, both the real and the imaginary parts of the quasi-normal frequencies decrease in absolute value. The overtone modes die out faster than the fundamental modes and no positive imaginary parts appear, indicating the stabilty of the system

</details>


### [18] [Relativistic effects in extreme-mass-ratio inspirals within scalar clouds: Eccentric and inclined orbits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21439)
*Qi-Xuan Xu,Richard Brito,Riccardo Della Monica,Rodrigo Vicente,Chen Yuan*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper investigates extreme-mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) in scalar cloud environments using a fully relativistic perturbative framework, extending prior work by incorporating eccentric and inclined orbits around a Schwarzschild black hole; it reveals resonance structures induced by apsidal precession, inclination-dependent energy transfer—including a critical inclination for cloud-to-orbit energy transfer—and persistent resonances across inclinations at high eccentricity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize relativistic studies of EMRIs in superradiant scalar clouds beyond circular, equatorial orbits—enabling realistic modeling for LISA and probing fundamental physics such as ultralight boson fields.

Method: Fully relativistic perturbative computation of scalar field energy and angular momentum fluxes (to infinity and horizon) for generic (eccentric, inclined) geodesic orbits in Schwarzschild spacetime; analysis of resonant orbital effects driven by relativistic apsidal precession.

Result: Eccentricity induces dense resonances near the last stable orbit via relativistic apsidal precession; inclination strongly modifies energy/angular momentum losses, with a critical inclination below which energy transfers *from* the scalar cloud *to* the orbit at small radii; high-eccentricity resonances persist across all inclinations but differ structurally between prograde and retrograde cases.

Conclusion: Generic orbital dynamics—eccentricity and inclination—introduce rich resonance and energy-transfer phenomenology in EMRIs embedded in scalar clouds, necessitating their inclusion in waveform modeling for LISA and laying groundwork for extension to Kerr black holes.

Abstract: We study extreme-mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) evolving in a scalar cloud environment that may form through superradiant instabilities, using a fully relativistic perturbative framework that allows for eccentric and inclined orbits. EMRIs, consisting of a stellar-mass compact object inspiraling into a supermassive black hole, are key sources for space-based gravitational-wave detectors such as LISA. Previous relativistic studies of EMRIs in scalar clouds have been restricted to circular, equatorial motion. Here, instead, we focus on a Schwarzschild black hole background to incorporate eccentricity and orbital inclination. By computing the scalar energy and angular momentum scattered off to spatial infinity and absorbed at the event horizon, we show that orbital eccentricity can induce a dense spectrum of resonances near the last stable orbit, associated with strong relativistic apsidal precession. We further find that orbital inclination can significantly modify the orbital energy and angular momentum losses. In particular, we identify a critical inclination angle below which, at sufficiently small orbital radii, there is a net transfer of energy from the scalar cloud to the orbit. Moreover, for sufficiently large eccentricities, resonances associated with relativistic apsidal precession persist across the full range of inclinations, although their structure changes significantly between prograde and retrograde orbits. These results provide a foundation for future studies of EMRIs in scalar cloud environments on fully generic orbits around spinning black holes.

</details>


### [19] [The WDM Time-Frequency Transform in Gravitational-Wave Data Analysis I: Formalism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21473)
*Aaron Johnson,Katerina Chatziioannou,Jake Summers*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper provides a self-contained, pedagogical introduction to time-frequency methods—specifically the Wilson-Daubechies-Meyer (WDM) basis—for gravitational-wave data analysis, emphasizing orthogonality, time-frequency localization, edge effects, transform implementation, and noise modeling in the time-frequency domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the knowledge gap for gravitational-wave analysts by systematically documenting time-frequency methods—particularly the WDM basis—which are underutilized despite offering a computationally efficient and flexible alternative to purely frequency- or time-domain approaches for slowly-varying noise.

Method: Derives and explicates the Wilson-Daubechies-Meyer (WDM) time-frequency basis; details forward/inverse transforms, orthogonality properties, time-frequency localization, treatment of DC/Nyquist edge effects, practical implementation strategies, and formulation of the noise covariance matrix and likelihood in the time-frequency domain.

Result: A comprehensive, accessible reference for gravitational-wave analysts on WDM-based time-frequency analysis, including validated implementation insights, characterization of basis properties, and a fully specified likelihood framework adapted to the time-frequency domain.

Conclusion: Time-frequency methods—especially the WDM basis—are viable, well-structured tools for gravitational-wave analysis of non-stationary, slowly-varying noise; this work establishes their theoretical foundation, practical usage, and statistical interpretation, promoting broader adoption and standardization.

Abstract: For slowly-varying noise, time-frequency methods offer a natural middle ground between the efficiency of the frequency domain and the generality of the more expensive time domain. Despite growing adoption, such methods remain less well documented and less familiar in the gravitational-wave literature, compared to the ubiquitous frequency domain. Aimed at gravitational-wave analysts, in this paper we present a self-contained account of time-frequency methods, detail derivations for a specific basis, namely the Wilson-Daubechies-Meyer (WDM) basis, and share intuition and lessons learned. We document key concepts: the orthogonality and good time-frequency localization of the basis, the edge effects at the DC and Nyquist frequencies, the forward and inverse transforms and their practical implementation, and the noise covariance matrix and likelihood in the time-frequency domain.

</details>


### [20] [Binary Black Hole Coalescence and the Dynamics of Scalar Hair in Einstein-Maxwell-Scalar Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21549)
*Jean Pierre Díaz,P. A. González,Eleftherios Papantonopoulos,Yerko Vásquez*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper numerically studies head-on collisions of charged black holes in Einstein-Maxwell-Scalar theory, showing that nonminimal electromagnetic-scalar coupling can dynamically generate scalar hair during merger—even from initially scalar-free black holes—and that the final state (scalarized vs. descalarized) depends critically on coupling strength and remnant charge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how scalar hair emerges or disappears dynamically in binary black hole mergers within Einstein-Maxwell-Scalar theory, particularly whether scalarization/descalarization transitions can occur in realistic binary scenarios.

Method: Numerical relativity simulations of head-on coalescence of charged binary black holes using puncture initial data (Reissner-Nordström black holes + kinetic scalar perturbation); full evolution of coupled Einstein-Maxwell-scalar equations; monitoring of apparent horizons, emitted radiation (gravitational and scalar), and horizon scalar field.

Result: Nonminimal coupling triggers dynamical scalar hair growth post-merger; final state bifurcates: weak coupling or charge cancellation leads to descalarization (radiation/absorption), while strong coupling + nonzero remnant charge yields stable scalarized black holes; scalar radiation waveform correlates with dominant gravitational-wave mode during nonlinear merger.

Conclusion: Scalarization/descalarization in EMS theory is a dynamical, merger-driven process governed by the interplay between electromagnetic-scalar coupling strength and the net charge of the final black hole—demonstrating that binary coalescence can realize and control hairy black hole formation.

Abstract: We investigate the head-on coalescence of charged binary black holes in Einstein-Maxwell-Scalar (EMS) theory using numerical relativity. The binaries are built from charged puncture initial data representing two Reissner-Nordström black holes immersed in a purely kinetic scalar perturbation: the scalar field initially vanishes, while its conjugate momentum provides a small seed for the instability. We evolve the coupled gravitational, electromagnetic, and scalar sectors and monitor the apparent horizons, the emitted radiation, and the scalar field on the horizons. Our simulations show that the nonminimal electromagnetic-scalar coupling can dynamically trigger the growth of scalar hair even when the individual black holes are initially scalar-free. The subsequent evolution depends on the coupling strength and on the charge retained by the remnant. For weak coupling, or when charge cancellation suppresses the electromagnetic source after merger, the scalar field is radiated away or absorbed by the final horizon and the system dynamically descalarizes. For sufficiently strong coupling and nonzero remnant charge, the scalar field remains finite and the final black hole approaches a scalarized configuration. The coalescence also excites scalar radiation whose time profile is qualitatively correlated with the dominant gravitational-wave mode during the nonlinear stage of the collision. These results provide a binary realization of scalarization/descalarization transitions in EMS theory and show that the fate of scalar hair is controlled by the interplay between the scalar coupling and the charge content of the remnant.

</details>


### [21] [Consistency Conditions and the Derivation of Harmonic Structure in Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21603)
*Bardia H. Fahim,Joshua G. Fenwick*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper derives the harmonic and conformal structure of Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton solutions from field equations in arbitrary dimensions, showing that generic solutions require equal dilaton couplings, Ricci-flat spatial base, and harmonic metric functions — unifying features across multi-center, cosmological, and black hole solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the common structural pattern—metric functions built from harmonic functions on a spatial base—observed in many exact solutions of Einstein-Maxwell and Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theories, without presupposing the base geometry or harmonic ansatz.

Method: Systematic analysis of the full Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton field equations in arbitrary dimensions with non-trivial dilaton-Maxwell coupling and Liouville-type potential; classification into generic and purely spatial branches; derivation of necessary constraints on couplings and geometry directly from equations.

Result: For the generic branch: dilaton-Maxwell and dilaton-potential couplings must be equal; spatial base must be Ricci-flat; metric function must be harmonic on that base; spacetime admits a conformal potential representation. For the spatial branch: distinct coupling constraint emerges.

Conclusion: The harmonic and conformal structures ubiquitous in known solutions (e.g., multi-center, cosmological, dynamical black holes) are not ad hoc ansätze but direct, necessary consequences of the field equations under generic assumptions.

Abstract: Many exact solutions of Einstein-Maxwell and Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theory share a common structural pattern in which the metric functions are built from harmonic functions on a specified spatial base, often taken to be flat. We investigate this pattern by considering the Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theory in arbitrary dimensions, where the dilaton field is non-trivially coupled to the Maxwell field and to a Liouville-type potential proportional to the cosmological parameter. Without imposing either the base geometry or the harmonic form of the metric function in advance, we show that for the generic branch, the field equations force the dilaton couplings to be equal, restrict the spatial base geometry to be Ricci flat, and make the metric function harmonic on this base. The resulting spacetime can then be written in terms of a conformal potential. We also consider a purely spatial branch, which instead leads to a distinct constraint on the coupling constants. These results provide a unified field-equation derivation of the harmonic behavior and conformal structure that appear in several classes of solutions, including multi-center geometries, cosmological solutions, and dynamical black holes.

</details>


### [22] [Mirror Symmetry and Double Signature Change](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21805)
*Tevian Dray,Charles Hellaby*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper reexamines the black mirror spacetime—a double-signature-change model connecting two Schwarzschild exteriors without an interior—using distributional geometry to test for horizon-located impulsive gravitational waves (i.e., surface-layer curvature singularities); it confirms their absence and reconciles this with analyticity claims, while analyzing global structure and causal behavior of worldlines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve conflicting claims about curvature regularity at the horizon of the black mirror spacetime and determine whether a distributional curvature singularity (impulsive gravitational wave) exists there, using rigorous geometric analysis adapted from signature-change formalism.

Method: Distributional geometry applied to the black mirror metric; analysis of curvature components in generalized functions sense; comparison with analytic continuation arguments; investigation of global causal structure and geodesic completeness across the horizon.

Result: No distributional curvature singularity (i.e., no surface layer or impulsive wave) is present at the horizon; curvature remains regular in the distributional sense, consistent with analyticity of curvature components in appropriate coordinates.

Conclusion: The black mirror spacetime is geometrically regular at the horizon—no impulsive gravitational wave or delta-function curvature layer exists—supporting its viability as a smooth double-signature-change model; global structure permits continuous, though non-differentiable, passage of timelike and null geodesics across the horizon.

Abstract: The black mirror spacetime proposed by Tzanavaris, Boyle, and Turok [1] connects the two exterior regions of the extended Schwarzschild black hole directly to each other, with no intervening interior region. Using techniques adapted from previous work on signature change, we reexamine the black mirror spacetime as a model of double signature change, and investigate whether there is a surface layer at the horizon, that is, a distributional curvature singularity corresponding to an impulsive gravitational wave. We confirm that the black mirror spacetime does not contain any such singularity, and compare our result with previous claims that the curvature components are analytic. We also discuss the global structure of the black mirror spacetime, and examine what happens to worldlines and curves passing through.

</details>


### [23] [Quantum Memory in Scalar-Induced Gravitational Waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21835)
*Waqas Ahmed*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that scalar-induced gravitational waves (GWs) retain residual quantum information—specifically anomalous coherence—even after scalar decoherence, and identifies tensor discord and connected power covariance as observable probes of primordial quantum coherence in secondary GW backgrounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard treatments model scalar-induced GWs as a classical stochastic background, ignoring potential residual quantum features (e.g., anomalous coherence, discord) that may persist after decoherence and carry information about the primordial quantum state.

Method: Starting from a decohered two-mode Gaussian scalar state, the authors derive exact transfer relations linking scalar anomalous coherence to the covariance matrix of induced tensor (gravitational-wave) modes; they analyze sourcing mechanisms for ordinary tensor power vs. opposite-mode tensor coherence, and compute tensor discord and connected tensor-power covariance.

Result: Scalar anomalous coherence sources opposite-mode tensor coherence—distinct from standard power sourcing—and generates nonzero tensor discord and connected tensor-power covariance even when scalar entanglement is lost; these quantities serve as measurable signatures of primordial quantum coherence.

Conclusion: Residual quantum coherence in scalar-induced GWs is physically detectable via phase-sensitive strain correlations and connected covariance in future GW observatories (e.g., LISA, PTA), challenging the purely classical stochastic paradigm and opening a new window into quantum aspects of inflationary perturbations.

Abstract: Scalar-induced gravitational waves are usually treated as a classical stochastic background sourced by phase-random curvature perturbations. We show that this description can miss residual quantum information. Starting from a decohered two-mode Gaussian scalar state, we derive explicit transfer relations between the scalar anomalous coherence and the covariance matrix of induced tensor modes. For a localized scalar power spectrum, the ordinary tensor power is sourced by scalar power contractions, whereas the opposite-mode tensor coherence is sourced by scalar anomalous-coherence contractions. This coherence can generate nonzero tensor discord and a connected tensor-power covariance even after scalar entanglement has vanished. We identify the connected covariance and phase-sensitive strain correlations as probes of primordial quantum coherence in secondary gravitational-wave backgrounds, and discuss their possible relevance for future space-based interferometers and pulsar timing arrays.

</details>


### [24] [Observational Constraints on $f(Q,T)$ Gravity in the Presence of DBI-Essence Scalar Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21839)
*Mayur Mune,Praveen Kumar Dhankar,Goutam Manna,Safiqul Islam,Bidisha Samanta,Behnam Pourhassan*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper explores late-time cosmology in f(Q,T) gravity coupled to a Dirac-Born-Infeld (DBI) scalar field, derives background equations for a flat FLRW universe, obtains analytic solutions for linear f(Q,T)=αQ+βT, and constrains parameters using H(z), DESI BAO, and Pantheon+SH0ES data—showing statistical compatibility with ΛCDM and potential relevance for H₀ tension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an alternative to ΛCDM that may alleviate observational tensions—particularly the H₀ discrepancy—by extending symmetric teleparallel gravity via non-metricity–matter coupling (f(Q,T)) and incorporating DBI dynamics.

Method: Derives field equations for f(Q,T)+DBI gravity on a flat FLRW background assuming an effective perfect fluid; solves analytically for linear f(Q,T)=αQ+βT; performs Bayesian parameter estimation using MCMC with Hubble rate (H(z)), DESI BAO (DR2), and Pantheon+SH0ES supernova data.

Result: Joint posterior constraints (Tables II & III) are broadly consistent with current late-time observations; the model is statistically viable versus ΛCDM, with deviations quantified by βT coupling and DBI contributions; it does not fully resolve all tensions but offers a flexible framework for further investigation.

Conclusion: f(Q,T)+DBI gravity constitutes a viable phenomenological extension of ΛCDM, capable of accommodating current cosmological data while offering new degrees of freedom—via non-metricity–matter coupling and DBI dynamics—to probe late-time cosmic acceleration and tension mitigation.

Abstract: We investigate late-time cosmology in extended symmetric teleparallel gravity coupled to a Dirac-Born-Infeld (DBI) scalar field within $f(Q,T)$ gravity, where $Q$ is the non-metricity scalar and $T$ is the trace of the matter energy-momentum tensor. Working on a spatially flat Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker background and treating the cosmic medium as an effective perfect fluid, we derive the background field equations for $f(Q,T)+\mathrm{DBI}$ gravity and obtain analytic solutions for the linear choice $f(Q,T)=αQ+βT$. We then constrain the model parameters with a Markov Chain Monte Carlo analysis using Hubble-rate data, DESI BAO (DR2) measurements, and the Pantheon+SHOES Type~Ia supernova sample. The joint posteriors (Tables II and III) are broadly consistent with current late-time constraints and allow a direct comparison with $Λ$CDM, quantifying the departures driven by the $βT$ coupling and the DBI sector. Although the model does not reproduce every observational feature exactly, it provides a statistically viable alternative avenue to the standard paradigm and a useful framework for exploring potential remedies to existing tensions, including the $H_0$ discrepancy, without claiming a definitive resolution.

</details>


### [25] [Entanglement, Discord, and Residual Coherence in Scalar-Induced Gravitational Waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21901)
*Waqas Ahmed*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper explores whether quantum coherence in primordial scalar perturbations—beyond entanglement—can imprint detectable non-Gaussian, phase-sensitive correlations in scalar-induced gravitational waves (GWs), using decohered two-mode squeezed states and covariance-matrix formalism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether residual quantum-informational features (e.g., anomalous scalar coherence) in the primordial curvature perturbations survive decoherence and manifest in induced gravitational waves, beyond standard classical stochastic modeling.

Method: Models primordial curvature perturbations as decohered two-mode squeezed Gaussian states; employs covariance-matrix formalism to track scalar coherence; derives scalar-to-tensor transfer relations for opposite-momentum tensor modes; identifies tensor coherence sourced by anomalous scalar contractions; computes connected observables like power covariance κ(k).

Result: Induced tensor modes exhibit coherence (not just power) sourced by anomalous scalar correlations; this generates Gaussian quantum discord and phase-sensitive, connected observables (e.g., κ(k) ∝ |γₖ|²/αₖ²); the signature is a correlated, nontrivially covariant GW background—not a spectral shift.

Conclusion: Scalar-induced gravitational waves can encode primordial quantum correlations beyond entanglement, offering a novel observational probe of residual quantum coherence in the early universe.

Abstract: Scalar-induced gravitational waves are usually modeled as a classical stochastic background sourced by primordial curvature perturbations. We investigate whether residual quantum-information properties of the scalar sector can survive decoherence and leave imprints in the induced tensor background. Using the covariance-matrix formalism, we describe primordial curvature perturbations as decohered two-mode squeezed Gaussian states and identify the anomalous scalar coherence that may remain after scalar entanglement has vanished. We then derive the leading scalar-to-tensor transfer relations for opposite-momentum induced tensor modes. The ordinary tensor power is sourced by scalar power contractions, whereas the opposite-mode tensor coherence is sourced by anomalous scalar-coherence contractions. This tensor coherence controls the induced Gaussian discord and generates connected and phase-sensitive observables, including a connected power covariance $κ(k)\propto |γ_k|^2/α_k^2$. Thus the robust signature is not a universal shift of the gravitational-wave spectrum, but a correlated tensor background with nontrivial covariance and phase structure. We discuss phenomenological templates and provide an illustrative Fisher estimate for future gravitational-wave observations. Our results suggest that scalar-induced gravitational waves may offer a new probe of primordial quantum correlations beyond entanglement.

</details>


### [26] [Determining Kerr black hole spin and inclination from a segment of the critical curve in black hole images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21921)
*Kenta Hioki,Umpei Miyamoto,Tomohiro Harada*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper proposes a method to infer Kerr black hole spin (a/M) and inclination (i) from localized segments of the critical curve, using three geometric observables derived from photon ring accumulation; it proves uniqueness within a defined domain and is designed for observational feasibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robust measurement of black hole spin and inclination from partial, realistic observations of photon rings—without requiring full reconstruction of the unobservable critical curve.

Method: Defines standardized critical curve segments and introduces three geometric observables characterizing their shape; analytically demonstrates injectivity of the mapping from (a/M, i, r_nl) to these observables in the non-extremal Kerr spacetime.

Result: The three observables uniquely determine the spin a/M, inclination i, and segment location parameter r_nl ∈ [0,1] within the considered domain, confirming that even fragmented critical curve data suffices for parameter inference.

Conclusion: Localized brightness features associated with photon rings encode sufficient geometric information to constrain black hole parameters; the framework is observationally practical and extendable to more general rotating black hole metrics.

Abstract: We present a method for determining the spin parameter $a/M$ and inclination angle $i$ of a non-extremal Kerr black hole from segments of the critical curve identified in black hole images. Although the critical curve itself is not directly observable, higher-order photon rings accumulate near it, and in realistic observations localized portions of the resulting brightness enhancement may be available for identifying segments of the critical curve. We introduce standardized segments of the critical curve and define three observables that characterize their geometry. We show that these observables uniquely determine $(a/M,i)$, together with an auxiliary parameter $r_{nl}\in[0,1]$ specifying the location of the identified segment along the critical curve, within the domain considered. Thus, even a segment of the critical curve contains sufficient geometric information to constrain the black hole spin and inclination without reconstructing the full critical curve. The framework is naturally suited to realistic observations and may be extended to more general rotating black hole spacetimes.

</details>


### [27] [Gravitational Radiation and Charges on de Sitter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22060)
*Emine Şeyma Kutluk*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper summarizes work on defining Bondi mass loss in Λ-BMS spacetimes by analyzing linearized Einstein equations in generalized harmonic gauge, linking metric perturbations to source multipole moments, connecting time-varying sources to gravitational memory, and defining charge flows via the holographic stress tensor at second order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Λ-BMS solution space lacks a straightforward analogue of Bondi mass loss; this paper addresses that gap by leveraging linearized gravity, multipole expansions, and holographic methods.

Method: Solves linearized Einstein equations in generalized harmonic gauge under adiabaticity and large-distance approximations; employs multipole expansion with consistent truncation; defines charge flows using the holographic stress tensor at second order in perturbations.

Result: Establishes a well-defined net change in metric perturbation at future null infinity tied to gravitational memory; derives explicit expressions for charge flows in terms of source multipole moments at second order.

Conclusion: The framework resolves the Bondi mass loss ambiguity in asymptotically de Sitter spacetimes, connects memory effects to observable charge flows, and provides a holographic interpretation consistent with Λ-BMS symmetry.

Abstract: We give a summary of our work [3] and [4] with Geoffrey Compère and Sk.Jahanur Hoque. After quickly reviewing the $Λ$-BMS solution space and why it is not trivial to define an analogue of Bondi mass loss, we discuss how this can be resolved by studying the linearized solutions. Then we describe how to solve the linearized Einstein s equations in the generalized harmonic gauge and the set of assumptions such as adiabaticity and being at a large distance from the source that allows us to write the metric perturbations in terms of the source multipole moments. We point out how to consistently truncate the multipole expansion. We then discuss the net change of the metric perturbation at future infinity due to a source that is time-varying in a finite time interval that will be connected to the memory effect.
